<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I have the retry dose-response N=5 results. Let me analyze:

**Retry N=5 aggregate:**
- 200 episodes, 10 death-prone tasks × 20
- Death rate: 87/200 = 43.5%
- Success rate: 22/200 = 11%
- Avg trials: most near 4.5-4.85 (close to max 5)

Now let me compare with existing data:

| Condition | Death Rate | N |
|-----------|-----------|---|
| Single-trial baseline | 42.9% | 245 (all 11 tasks) |


| Retry (3×) | 40.9% | 220 (10 death-prone) |
| Retry (5×) | 43.5% | 200 (10 death-prone) |

This is concerning—the N=5 retry actually shows a higher death rate than both the baseline and the N=3 retry, which suggests that more attempts aren't helping. The issue is that the baseline includes find-non-living-thing with 0% death rate, while the N=5 condition only covers the 10 death-prone tasks, so the comparison isn't quite apples-to-apples.

Looking at the paper's Table 1 more carefully, the N=3 retry was run across all 11 tasks with 20 episodes each (11 × 20 = 220), while the N=5 retry appears to be limited to just the 10 death-prone tasks. This explains the discrepancy in the sample sizes and why the death rates look so different. Looking back at the experiments file, the retry comparison shows 40.9% versus 42.9% baseline with only marginal difference. The N=220 likely represents 11 tasks across 20 episodes each, but this particular experiment only collected N=5 data according to the summary. The earlier N=1 through N=3 results must be from the original retry baseline experiment, so this dose-response curve was supposed to span N=1 to N=5 but only captured the N=5 endpoint.

For the actual comparison, I need to isolate the death-prone tasks by excluding find-non-living-thing from the baseline calculation. The paper reports 42.9% overall (105 deaths out of 245 episodes), but accounting for find-non-living-thing's varying episode counts across the primary data makes this calculation more involved. The key takeaway is that retry at N=5 shows a death rate around 43.5%.

My hypothesis predicted the death rate would plateau between 35-40% at N=5, with the difference between N=5 and N=3 staying under 3 percentage points. But 43.5% is higher than expected. If N=3 was around 40.9% on all tasks, it might be closer to 45% on just the death-prone ones, making the N=5 versus N=3 gap small and confirming a plateau—just at a higher absolute level than I anticipated. Something seems off though, since the summary only shows N=5 results without the intermediate N=1 through N=4 comparisons that would validate the plateau hypothesis.

Looking at the experiment spec, `--n-values 5` likely means each episode runs 5 retry trials, so I should be able to extract death rates for N=1, 2, 3, and 4 from the same dataset by checking whether death occurred within the first K trials. The summary format shows per-task data under `"conditions": {"n5": {...}}`, which suggests the raw episode files might contain trial-by-trial information I can use to reconstruct the full N=1..5 curve. Let me examine one of the individual episode files to see how the data is structured.
```

_source line 3504 · 2026-05-18T12:45:44.722Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am the Research Reviewer (独立科学审查员) for the "simgate-modular-routing" project targeting EMNLP 2026 (DDL 2026-05-25). This session continued from a previous context-compacted conversation. The primary tasks were:
   - Complete the seed-123 replication audit (computing DAF, Δ_full, Δ_nd, and posting to Director)
   - Verify all paper text fixes from the earlier claim-data consistency audit
   - Monitor running experiments (retry dose-response N=5, reflexion dose-response)
   - Audit the retry-dose-response experiment upon completion (most recent task)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Death Avoidance Factor (DAF)**: DAF = 1 - Δ_nd/Δ_full ≈ δP/(δP + Δ_nd), measures fraction of routing signal from death avoidance
   - **Death-asymmetry label fraction**: Penalty-independent metric; fraction of non-tie routing labels from death asymmetry
   - **Episode-level counterfactual labeling**: Comparing pure-strategy outcomes (internal vs deterministic) per episode
   - **Dose-response experiment**: Running N=1..5 retry/reflexion trials to show death rate plateau
   - **Grep pattern contamination**: `grep "internal_final.*-100"` falsely matches -100 in OTHER fields (det_final, score_gap) on the same JSON line; correct pattern is `grep '"internal_final": -100'`
   - **Score gap**: score_gap = det_final - int_final per episode; sum/N = Δ_full
   - **ssh_read_only restrictions**: Blocks `|`, `>`, `<`, `open()` in python -c; only allows cat/grep/ls/find/head/tail/wc/stat

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`docs/paper/discussion.tex`** (24 lines):
     - Verified all fixes from earlier audit are applied
     - L11: "All 105 internal-strategy deaths" ✓ (was 73)
     - L11: "both strategies exhibit comparable focus on rates (~5%)" ✓ (was "never executes focus on")
     - L11: "workshop remains the most frequent (20.0%)" ✓ (was 44.6%)
     - L11: "inventory (17.1%) and kitchen (10.5%)" ✓
     - No em-dash or AI writing style issues noted

   - **`docs/paper/experiments.tex`** (124 lines):
     - L24: CI "[73.0%, 100%]" ✓ (was [73.3%, 100%])
     - L24: "wild cluster bootstrap (K=11 task clusters, B=9,999) confirms significance (p < 0.001, Cohen's d = 3.02)"
     - L26-27: NEW "Seed robustness" paragraph with seed=123 data:
       - "DAF = 89.7%, within 0.7 pp of the primary analysis (90.4%)"
       - "death-asymmetry label fraction rises from 78.9% (seed=42) to 86.5% (seed=123)"
       - "Δ_nd = 2.83 vs. 2.84"
       - "Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly (45.5% vs. 42.9% and 20.9% vs. 16.3%)"
       - "per-task death-rate correlation r = 0.94"
     - L55: "The 31.4% in Table 1 covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ~35%, the figure reported in the Abstract" ✓
     - All numbers match my independent analysis

   - **`docs/paper/abstract.tex`** (3 lines):

   - **`docs/paper/main.tex`** (appendix sections, read L355-384):

   - **Remote: `westd-18266:/root/autodl-tmp/simgate-modular-routing/results/seed123-plan-c/`**:

```json
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\%), while $10{\\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates on death-prone tasks to ${\\sim}$35\\%, yet none closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "ced48dd9-6d40-4906-8ada-d9f11e6eb59f",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$; Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states. Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",
        "numLines": 24,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 24
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/discussion.tex"
```

### Assistant

_source line 3538 · 2026-05-18T12:46:32.807Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I understand the data structure. Each episode file has a `trials` array with entries for trial_idx 0-4. Each trial has `is_death` and `is_success` fields.

For constructing the dose-response curve:
- N=1: death if trial 0 is death (and no success)
- N=2: death if ALL of trials 0-1 result in death (none succeed)
- Actually wait. "Retry" means you retry if the first attempt fails. The episode-level outcome under N retries: the episode "dies" if ALL N trials result in death. The episode "succeeds" if ANY trial succeeds.


- The retry logic stops at the first success, so the final outcome is determined by whether any of the N trials succeeded, or if all N trials resulted in death. Looking at the data structure, `final_is_death` is true when all trials die, and `success_trial` is null when no trial succeeds.

Now I need to verify this pattern by examining episodes where some trials don't die, particularly looking at the grow-fruit dataset which shows mixed outcomes, to confirm the death rates at each trial count N.
```

</details>

```text
Good. This episode has all 5 trials with `is_death: false` and `is_success: false` (score 0.0 each). So this is a non-death, non-success episode. The agent just fumbles around with no catastrophic failure and no success.

Now I understand the key insight: for the dose-response curve at N=K, an episode "dies" iff ALL of trials 0..K-1 have `is_death: true`. So:
- N=1: episode dies if trial[0].is_death = true


- N=2: episode dies if trial[0].is_death = true AND trial[1].is_death = true
- N=3: episode dies if trial[0].is_death AND trial[1].is_death AND trial[2].is_death
- N=4: same for trials 0-3
- N=5: same for trials 0-4

And an episode succeeds at N=K if ANY of trials 0..K-1 has `is_success: true`. So the `final_is_death` at N=5 is true only if all 5 trials are deaths.

Now I need to extract the per-trial `is_death` values from all 200 episode files.

Since ssh_read_only blocks pipes and Python file operations, I'll need to use grep patterns to count episodes where specific trials have death outcomes. The challenge is that each file contains 5 trials with nested `is_death` fields, so I need to figure out how to isolate and count deaths at each trial index for the dose-response calculation. trial[1] is NOT death. So I can compute N=2 by subtracting episodes where trial[0] is death but trial[1] isn't from the N=1 count.

Given the read-only constraints, I need to think about what's actually observable in the data. Let me check whether trials only run if the previous one failed—if trial 0 succeeds, do they skip trials 1-4? Looking at a sample file, trial 0 has a score of 0, which suggests...

Actually, examining the data more carefully: all 5 trials always run regardless of outcome. Even when all trials are deaths, or when none succeed, the full sequence 0-4 executes. This means I can compute N=K retrospectively by checking if all trials 0 through K-1 are deaths—that's when the episode dies at retry count K.

Let me search through the episode files to identify the death patterns. For each file, I'll count consecutive deaths starting from trial 0, since the retry logic keeps attempting until either success or exhaustion. If trials 0 through D-1 are all deaths but trial D isn't, then the episode dies at N≤D but survives at N>D.

Actually, the `condition_summary.json` file might already have the aggregated per-N survival data I need, so let me check that first instead of parsing individual episode files.
```

```text
The condition_summary.json only has N=5 data. I need to extract per-trial data from individual episodes to construct N=1..4.

Since ssh_read_only is restricted, let me try a python approach to extract is_death for each trial in each file. I can use `python3 -c` with only print statements.

Actually, wait. Let me try using `grep` creatively. In the JSON files, the trials are in order, and each trial block contains "is_death": true/false. I need to find, for each file, whether all the first K trials are deaths.
 
I'm realizing the JSON structure makes line-based grep tricky since trial_idx and is_death are separated by several lines. Let me count the total occurrences of "is_death": true in each file—if all five trials are deaths, the episode fails at every N level, but if there are fewer deaths, I need to figure out which trial actually survives.

The real insight is that what matters for the dose-response curve is identifying the first trial where death doesn't occur. A simple count won't tell me that—I need to extract the actual sequence of is_death values per trial. Let me write a script to parse the JSON properly and track which trial index corresponds to the first non-death outcome. I'm realizing I need to extract the first non-death trial for each episode across all 200 files. Using find with grep might work if the ssh tool allows it, though the output could be unwieldy. What I really need is to identify the first escape trial per episode—the first trial where is_death is false—then calculate the death rate at each dose level N based on how many episodes never escape by trial N. I'm realizing that approach might generate too much output. Let me think about this differently—since trial indices are ordered 0-4 within each file, I can use grep to count deaths per file, then cross-reference with the sequential structure. But actually, that won't work because deaths across trials aren't correlated; each trial is an independent retry with different environment randomness, so I can't assume patterns.

Looking at the actual data though, I'm noticing something interesting: in chemistry-mix_ep000, all 5 trials end in death with the same "focus on [object]" message, while grow-fruit_ep001 has all 5 trials running without death and all scoring zero. This suggests outcomes might be consistent within a given episode. Let me verify whether any episode has mixed results—some trials dying and others not. For grow-fruit specifically, 10 out of 20 episodes result in death at N=5, and the average trials metric is 4.0, which is less than 5. That could indicate early stopping when success is achieved. Looking at grow-fruit_ep001 more closely, all 5 trials completed, none resulted in death, and all had zero score.
```

### Assistant

_source line 3568 · 2026-05-18T12:48:30.535Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
So when an episode succeeds at trial 0, the experiment stops early - only 1 trial runs (`num_trials: 1`). This is early stopping on success. 

This means for the dose-response computation:
- If success_trial = K, the episode ran K+1 trials, and the last trial succeeded. Earlier trials may or may not have died.
- If success_trial = null, all 5 trials ran, and the final outcome is determined by final_is_death.

For computing N=K death rate:
- If success_trial = S < K: the episode is NOT a death at N=K (it succeeded before K trials)


- If success_trial = S >= K or success_trial = null: I need to check the first K trials' outcomes. If all K trials ended in death, the episode dies at N=K; if any trial before K succeeded or survived, the episode survives at N=K.

The tricky part is that for early-stopped episodes, I only have data for trials 0 through S, so trials after S don't exist in the data.

For episodes that succeeded early at trial S, I need to trace back what happened on trials 0 through S-1 to determine the death status at each N. If S=0, the episode never dies. If S=1, trial 0 might have been death, but the episode succeeds once trial 1 completes. I need to extract the per-trial is_death and is_success status for each episode to properly compute the dose-response at each N.

Now I'm going to batch-extract the key fields from all 200 files using grep to pull num_trials, success_trial, final_is_death, and the per-trial is_death sequences. For episodes with num_trials=5 and no early success, I can read the 5 is_death values directly. For episodes with early success, I need to check the is_death status of trials before the success point to determine when the episode would have died.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I see! ep012 has:
- Trial 0: death
- Trial 1: death
- Trial 2: NON-death
- Trial 3: death
- Trial 4: death
- final_is_death: true

So `final_is_death` = true because the LAST trial (trial 4) died. It's NOT "all trials die" but rather "the last attempted trial died."

This means the definition of "death at N=5" is: the last trial (trial 4) resulted in death, regardless of intermediate non-death trials.
 
Hmm, but that doesn't align with how a retry mechanism should work. In a proper retry system, you'd keep attempting until you get a success, then stop. So if trial 2 succeeded, you wouldn't run trials 3 and 4. The fact that we have a trial 4 that died suggests the mechanism is just running all trials regardless, and the final outcome is whatever happened on the last one.

Let me think about this differently. The final outcome across all retries depends on whether any trial succeeded. If at least one trial succeeded, the episode ends in success. If none succeeded, then the final outcome is whatever the last trial produced. So for an episode with N=5 retries, if trials 0-3 all failed and trial 4 died, then final_is_death would be true. But if trial 2 didn't die (just scored 0 without succeeding), that doesn't matter—we're only looking at whether the final outcome is death, which is determined by whether we ever got a success or what the last trial did.

This creates an odd situation for the dose-response curve: the death rate at N=K would be the fraction of episodes where no trial succeeded in attempts 0 through K-1, and trial K-1 specifically resulted in death. But intuitively, if you're retrying to avoid death, you'd want to count yourself as safe if any single attempt avoids death, not just the final one. So the final outcome logic appears to be: if any trial succeeds, use that success; otherwise, report the last trial's outcome. This means for the dose-response analysis, a "death" at N=K trials means no success was found AND the final attempt died—which is different from saying all K trials died. I'm realizing this interpretation might skew the results compared to a standard dose-response where you'd count either all failures or the best outcome across attempts. Let me check what the baseline death rate actually is for the same task subset to see if the retry comparison is even valid. death rate on 10 death-prone tasks (excluding find-non-living-thing)? The baseline has 105 deaths / 245 episodes total. On the 10 death-prone tasks, that's 105 deaths / (245 - ~22) = 105/223 ≈ 47.1%. Hmm, but I corrected this earlier: 100 internal deaths, not 105. So 100/220 = 45.5%? No wait, the seed 42 main data has 11 tasks × ~22 episodes = ~242 total, with 105 deaths. Actually, I remember the main dataset: 245 episodes across 11 tasks, internal deaths count was... let me not rely on memory.

The comparison should be between the baseline N=1 death rate on the 10 death-prone tasks from the dose-response data itself versus the N=5 death rate. With the experiment's `--n-values 5` setup, N=1 means looking only at the first trial, while N=5 means looking at all 5 trials. The death rate for N=K should represent the fraction of episodes where, given only K retries, the outcome is death—running trials sequentially and stopping on first success, otherwise taking the final trial's result. at K = no success in 0..K-1 AND trial[K-1].is_death

So for the dose-response:
- N=1: trial[0].is_death (if trial 0 doesn't succeed, which it might)
  Actually: N=1 means you run 1 trial. If trial 0 succeeds → success. If trial 0 dies → death. If trial 0 neither → non-death-non-success.
  Death at N=1 = trial[0].is_death

- N=2: run trials 0-1. If trial 0 succeeds → success. If trial 0 fails, try trial 1. If trial 1 succeeds → success. Otherwise outcome = trial[1].
  But wait, trials 0 and 1 are independent retries with the same environment seed? Or different seeds?

Actually, looking at the code from registry: the seed applies to episode selection, not to the agent's behavior. Each trial gets different randomness from the LLM. Under "last trial" semantics, death at N=K means no success in trials 0 through K-1, and trial K-1 results in death. This creates an interesting problem—for episodes where later trials are death but earlier ones aren't, the dose-response curve could actually be non-monotonic. Death at N=3 might not occur if trial 2 survives, but death at N=5 could happen if trial 4 dies.

Let me verify this against the actual episode data. For ep012 with trials [death, death, non-death, death, death] and final_is_death=true, the "last trial" interpretation fits: the final outcome is determined by trial 4's death. I should check another episode to confirm whether this is truly the governing logic or if there's a majority-vote or any-success rule at play.
```

_source line 3606 · 2026-05-18T12:51:46.470Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Confirmed! find-animal_ep016:
- Trial 0: non-death
- Trial 1: death
- Trial 2: non-death
- Trial 3: non-death
- Trial 4: death
- final_is_death: true (even though trials 0, 2, 3 are non-death!)

This confirms "last trial" semantics: `final_is_death = trial[4].is_death`.

This is very important. It means `final_is_death` doesn't mean "the episode always dies across all retries." It means "the last retry attempt resulted in death."


For the dose-response curve, this distinction is critical. The 43.5% death rate at N=5 doesn't represent episodes that inevitably fail—it's just the proportion where the final attempt ended in death. But this creates a conceptual problem: if a retry is supposed to help, shouldn't we count it as successful whenever ANY trial avoids death, rather than only looking at the last one? Under this "last trial" semantics, an episode could succeed on trial 2 but still be marked as a failure if trial 4 dies, which seems to undercount the actual benefit of retrying.

This raises a serious question about what the paper's claims actually demonstrate. The "marginal reduction" finding would just be comparing the baseline death rate to the final trial's death rate—and if individual trials are independent and identically distributed, that final rate should stay roughly constant across different N values, trivially confirming no improvement for a fundamentally flawed reason. Similarly, the 40.9% figure from the retry-control baseline might just be reflecting the third trial's death rate, which should resemble the baseline anyway.

This could be a major issue: if the retry mechanism is only recording the last outcome instead of the best one, the experiment isn't actually testing whether retrying helps. Though there's apparently an early-stopping mechanism when a trial succeeds, which would change things.

Let me think through this more carefully. If success triggers an immediate stop but failure requires running all K trials, then the death rate gets computed differently—it's only counting episodes where all K trials failed and the final one resulted in death, divided by the total episode count. This means the denominator includes episodes that succeeded earlier, which would artificially lower the reported death rate even if individual trial outcomes haven't improved.

But here's the wrinkle: if an episode succeeds on trial 2 but would have died on trial 4, that death never gets recorded at N=5 because the episode already stopped. So the dose-response curve could actually be non-monotonic depending on how these outcomes distribute across trials. This is getting complicated.

Let me reconsider whether the paper's definition actually makes sense. Maybe the "retry" setup works differently—perhaps the final outcome is just the best result across all K trials, where success beats non-death beats death. Under that logic, ep012 with trials D,D,ND,D,D should show as non-death since at least one trial didn't die. But the data clearly marks it as death, so either my interpretation is wrong or there's a bug in the implementation.

Let me trace through ep012's actual trial data more carefully to see what's really happening.
```

````text
    accumulated_reflections = []

        # ... run episode ...

        is_success = ep["final_score"] > 0 and not ep["is_death"]


```

Building the trial records...

I'm tracking whether each trial succeeds, and if it does, I break early—so the trials aren't fully independent when calculating death rates. The final metrics pull from the last trial in the sequence, but I also need to capture which trial (if any) actually succeeded. The key insight here is that the retry mechanism doesn't test "more chances reduce death"—it just tests whether the final independent attempt dies, though early stopping on success does create a dependency in the death rate calculation. I should distinguish between two different ways to measure the impact of retries: the "last trial" approach that was actually implemented, where only the final attempt counts, versus a "best-of-K" approach where you could pick the best outcome across all attempts. The paper's 40.9% reduction figure used the last-trial semantics, so I need to compute both metrics to see how much the measurement choice matters. The "last trial" semantics reflects how retry actually works in practice—using the final outcome rather than picking retroactively—but the "best-of-K" metric is more revealing for understanding whether death is fundamentally structural or just stochastic. If even the best outcome across K attempts still shows high death rates, that would suggest the problem is structural rather than just noise.

Now I need to extract the per-trial death data to compute both metrics. For the last-trial approach at each N, I can use the final trial's death status for each episode, and for the best-of-K complement, I need to check whether all trials across the K attempts resulted in death. Let me pull the per-trial data from the files and process it locally. I need to be more careful about distinguishing trial-level `is_death` entries from the episode-level `final_is_death` field. The indentation should help—trial entries have 6 spaces while the final one has 2. Let me just sample a few representative episodes per task and manually compute the death probabilities, since 200 episodes should give me stable aggregate counts anyway. For the dose-response analysis, I really just need to count how many episodes have all trials resulting in death at each N value. I need to define what "dies at N" means precisely: an episode dies at N if all trials from 0 through N-1 are deaths, which means for early-success episodes where success happens at trial S, the episode can only die at N values up to S (since trial S itself is a success, not a death). So I'm checking each N by verifying that all preceding trials are marked as deaths, stopping at whichever comes first—either reaching 5 trials or hitting the success trial. Now I'm seeing the pattern clearly—for episodes that succeeded early, I only have trial data up to that success point, but for the full 5-trial runs I have complete outcome data. Since only about 11% of episodes stopped early, most of my data is complete. I'll extract the success_trial values and per-trial death outcomes across all 200 files, then compute the metrics locally with bulk operations.
````

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have success_trial for all 200 episodes. Let me compile:

Success episodes (22 total, matching summary):
1. chemistry-mix: ep002(0), ep008(0), ep009(3) = 3 successes
2. find-animal: ep002(0), ep008(0) = 2 successes
3. freeze: ep011(0) = 1 success
4. grow-fruit: ep002(0), ep006(0), ep009(0), ep011(0), ep019(0) = 5 successes


5. identify-life-stages-1: ep003(1) = 1 success
6. identify-life-stages-2: ep003(0) = 1 success
7. inclined-plane: ep005(3), ep015(2) = 2 successes
8. lifespan-longest-lived: ep016(0), ep017(1) = 2 successes
9. lifespan-l-then-s: ep003(1), ep016(4), ep017(4) = 3 successes
10. measure-melting-point: ep002(0), ep011(0) = 2 successes

That brings me to 22 total successes across these tasks.

Now I'm looking at the episodes where success didn't happen on the first trial—these are the cases worth examining more closely. I need to check whether there were any deaths recorded before the successful trial for each of these 8 episodes: chemistry-mix ep009, identify-life-stages-1 ep003, inclined-plane ep005 and ep015, lifespan-longest-lived ep017, and lifespan-l-then-s ep003, ep016, and ep017.

I already have chemistry-mix ep009 figured out—it shows deaths on trials 1, 2, and 3 before succeeding on trial 4. For the remaining 7 episodes, I need to pull their per-trial death data to understand the pattern. But before diving into all the details, I should clarify what matters for the dose-response analysis: under the "last trial" semantics, at N=1 an episode counts as a death only if the first trial was marked as death AND there was no success on that trial. Since success requires a positive score and absence of death, if the first trial is marked as death, success is impossible there anyway.

For episodes with no success across all trials, the death status at any N simply comes from whether trial N-1 was marked as death. But for the 22 episodes that do succeed, I need to split them: the 15 that succeed immediately on trial 0 never count as deaths at any N, while the 7 that succeed later need their death status determined by trial N-1 up until they reach their success point, after which they're no longer counted as deaths.

The 7 episodes with delayed success are really the only edge case here, and since they only matter for N values up to around 3 or 4, they contribute minimally to the overall pattern—probably less than 4% of the 200 total episodes. This means the dose-response curve should be almost entirely shaped by the 178 non-success episodes, where death at N=K is simply determined by whether trial K-1 was a death. If each trial has roughly the same underlying death probability, then the death rate should stay fairly constant across different N values for these episodes. Let me check what fraction of trials across all positions are actually marked as deaths in the non-success group.

For chemistry-mix specifically, I'm looking at the 17 non-success episodes out of 20 total, each with 5 trials. Tallying up the death counts across these episodes: 5, 0, 5, 5, 5, 0, 5, 0, 0, 4, 0, 0, 5, 5, 5, and a few more I need to recount to make sure I have the right total.

Let me recalculate: that's 49 deaths across 85 total trial slots, giving a per-trial death rate of about 57.6% for the non-success episodes. The overall death rate for chemistry-mix is lower at 50% because the 3 successful episodes don't contribute to the death count. Now I'm stepping back to focus on what matters most for the audit rather than getting lost in these granular calculations.

The core issue is that the experiment only ran at N=5, not across N=1 through N=5 separately. The `final_is_death` metric uses "last trial" semantics—specifically looking at whether trial 4 resulted in death. For the paper's claim about retry yielding marginal reduction at 40.9%, that figure comes from the earlier N=3 baseline, while the new experiment shows 43.5% at N=5. Both are using the same "last trial" semantics, so I can compare them: the N=1 baseline sits around 42.9%, N=3 is 40.9%, and N=5 is 43.5%. This means the death rate under "last trial" semantics actually increases slightly as N grows.

The reason is straightforward: each trial has roughly the same underlying death probability, and "last trial" semantics just measures that constant probability. But this reveals a critical flaw in the experimental design. If we were instead using "best-of-K" semantics—where an episode only dies if all K trials fail—the death rate would drop geometrically. With a per-trial death probability around 50%, the probability that all 5 trials die would be roughly 0.50^5, or about 3.1%. That would be a massive reduction, not marginal at all.

Now I'm checking whether the trials are actually independent by looking at the raw data. I'm seeing cases like chemistry-mix episode 000 where all 5 trials die with the same focus pattern, which suggests the trials might not be independent after all.

Comparing the observed distribution against what we'd expect if trials were truly independent with P(death)=0.50: we should see about 3% of episodes with all 5 deaths and 3% with zero deaths, but instead we're observing 53% with all 5 deaths and 41% with zero deaths. This massive skew toward extreme outcomes strongly suggests the trials are highly correlated rather than independent.

The episode itself—its particular environment state and the LLM's approach to it—appears to determine the outcome far more than random variation between trials. This means best-of-K selection won't help much on episodes where all trials fail, since they're likely to fail regardless of which trial we pick. Only the small fraction of episodes with mixed outcomes would actually benefit from trying multiple times.

To verify this matters, I need to count how many episodes across all tasks have this mixed pattern, then check whether the "last trial" and "best-of-K" death rates actually differ meaningfully. If the bimodal structure dominates, the difference should be small, which would suggest the main finding holds regardless of which metric we use.
```
